The rain pools along the door stones' edges are now real water that takes reflections instead of black mud; In rain the door stones now go dark and glassy, water filming their worn tops and lying in pools along their edges; The door stones are now broad, thin, flat-topped field flags laid across the track, their split edges plumb and their walked tops a clouded warm buff, with dark dirt packed into the crack round each one; The tall grass no longer leans over the door stones and their walked tops are worn pale and clean; No grass grows through the door stones any more, and the hero's feet now knock dry on the slabs -- a short heel-and-toe click with a brief ring of stone -- or slap wet on them in rain; The bat can now be heard: a thin, high trill of chirps every few seconds as it loops the lantern, a rattle of clicks closing to a buzz each time it stoops on a moth, and a dry grumble from under the woodshed eaves when it stretches by day; and its fur and wrapped wings no longer glow orange in the shade; Each house door now has flat stepping stones worn into the track outside it: three or four split field slabs a pace apart, a lip proud of the dirt, the last half lost where the track runs on; The bat now has a home: by day it hangs head down under the white cottage's woodshed boards between the rafters, wings wrapped, swaying in the draught and now and then stretching and shivering a wing; at dusk it lets go, drops, opens its wings and flies out of the open end of the lean-to to the lantern, and at first light or when rain drives it in it flies back under the eaves and hangs up again, coming out once more when the rain has passed; A pipistrelle bat now hawks the moths round the lantern on the green after dark: it comes in out of the night once the candle is burning, beats a fast jinking loop a few metres round the light, and every few seconds stoops at a moth; about a third of the time it takes it, otherwise the moth hears it, folds and drops out from under it and the bat swings back round; it keeps off the lantern and the turf, and leaves in rain, strong wind and before dawn; The village at night now lights properly: the lantern post throws a soft, wide amber pool over the track, the grass and the timber house front instead of a pale yellow one, and fireflies drifting past the white cottage no longer paint a 2 m lime disc on its limewash; Edda now has a voice of her own at work and rest: a short breathed "hah" as each axe blow comes down at the block, and out on her bench of an evening she hums a slow folk phrase to herself every ten seconds or so, falling quiet when the hero comes near; Edda now calls the hens as she brings the grain bag round the walls ("chook-chook-chook", a sung woman's call every ~3 s from ~20 m out), and the hens leave off what they are doing, look up, hurry to the inside of the east wall and wait there in a line, heads turning toward her, until the first handful flies; Edda now talks: walk up to her and press E to speak; she introduces herself, then has lines for what she is about (the butt, the hens, the woodpile, her bench, the lantern she lights every night for her husband Tam who went up to the keep and never came down), holding still and facing the hero while each line shows; Edda now feeds the hens twice a day: at 07:20 and 16:20 she takes a linen grain bag round the outside of the yard walls to the garth's east wall and casts six handfuls over it; the grain patters down and lies on the scratched earth, and the hens run to it with their wings half out and peck it up grain by grain; Edda now takes her pail up from beside the water butt, carries it hung by its iron bail, dips it full and sets it down again before she goes on to the woodpile; Edda now splits wood at the chopping block through the morning and afternoon: she stoops to gather the last halves and set a fresh round on the block, heaves a real axe (ash helve with a knob swell, forged head with a bright ground bit) up over her head with both hands and brings it down; the round splits and the halves jump off to either side in a spray of chips with a bite-and-crack, or one blow in four sticks with a dull thud and she swings again; the axe goes back into the block when she leaves; After rain the trodden tracks across the green now hold water: the scuffed dips down their packed middles fill first, then a chain of long thin pools with slick mud at their edges, and the hero splashes through them; Edda, the village's first cottager, now keeps a day at the white cottage: out of the door at six, stooping to the water butt and the woodpile through the morning and afternoon, sat on the bench under her window at noon, walking out at dusk with a rush taper to light the lantern on the green (which now stays dark until she has), an evening on the bench, in at the door for the night; a rain front sends her indoors until it passes and she stops to turn and watch the hero when he comes close; Beaten-earth footpaths now cross the green from each house door to the lantern post where they meet, and one runs on from there toward the start, narrowing and breaking up into the sward as it reaches the meadow; Moths now come to the lantern on the green at night: from dusk they fly in out of the dark one by one -- grey-brown heart and darts, ivory white ermines peppered black, brown large yellow underwings flashing ochre hindwings -- loop round the lit horn panes in erratic circles, dart off and come back, blunder into the panes, and now and then settle on the post's face under the arm with their wings folded back before taking off again; rain and a storm wind keep most of them away and at first light they drift off; An oak lantern post now stands where the tracks meet on the green: a chamfered post with a weathering cap, a braced arm reaching toward the village centre and a black wrought-iron lantern with horn panes hung on a short chain; from dusk to first light a tallow candle burns in it, the flame flickers, its warm light pools on the grass and walls, and the lantern sways on its chain with the wind so the light and the frame's shadows move; The stone cottage is now walled in split grey field stone on beds of uneven height -- thin snecking courses, deep courses of big stones, ragged tops with the lime mortar showing -- with long dressed weathered-sandstone quoins at the corners and a field-stone chimney stack; A small feather-boarded oak hen house now stands on posts in the garth's back-west corner with a tarred pent roof, a nest box under a lift-up lid and a slatted ladder up to its pop-hole: at dusk the hens gather at the ladder's foot and go up it one at a time into the house, and at first light they come down it one after another; The hens' garth is now scratched down to dry bare earth -- pale dust and packed loam, claw-rake furrows with the dust kicked out behind, dust-bath hollows, pea grit, droppings and moulted feathers, ragged tufts surviving toward the walls -- and a beaten footpath runs from the garth gate round the east gable, in through the yard wall's squeeze gap to the cottage door, and from the door out of the cart gateway toward the green (grass and flowers keep off it); Five hens of mixed plumage (brown, black, cuckoo, buff, partridge) now keep the garth behind the stone cottage -- they potter about in short jerky steps with the head held still then thrust, scratch the turf back with one foot and the other and peck at it, cluck to themselves, run squawking with their wings half out when you walk up on one (never out of the garth), stand out heavy rain under the back eaves fluffed up, and settle along the back wall at dusk until first light; the garth is scratched short and patchy (no tall grass or flowers inside it, `Hens.scratched`); The yard and garth walls now look like field walls rather than brickwork -- big half-bedded footing boulders, courses of mixed heights that rise and dip along the wall, short stones packed with pinning wedges, taller copes leaning one way down a stretch and then the other, cool grey gritstone with grey lichen and moss only on the tops, the joints and the shaded faces; Dry-stone walls now close the yard between the two houses -- rough split field stones on a batter, through-stones and a row of cope stones on edge, grey with lichen and moss low down -- with a cart gateway between two tall gate stones toward the green and a squeeze gap on the far side; An oak five-bar field gate now hangs in the garth wall's gateway on iron strap hinges -- walk up to it and it swings away from you, and once you are through it falls shut by its own weight with a dry hinge creak and a wooden knock; A dry-stone garth wall now closes a yard behind the stone cottage -- battered rubble faces, through-stones, an on-edge cope, a gateway between two upright stoops on the east side you can walk through; A second house, a stone cottage, stands across the green north of the timber one, its door also turned toward you -- coursed rubble walls with long corner quoins, oak lintels, a tarred plank door standing open, leaded windows with open shutters and stone sills, a 42-degree roof of graded riven slates with lichen and moss toward the eaves, a stone ridge and a chimney stack trailing smoke; you can walk up the step and in at the door, and from dusk to bedtime its windows glow and a lamp lights the room; When the fox sits, its brush (now 0.5 m long) lies on the turf out of the rump and curls round its right haunch with the tip beside its forepaws, instead of sweeping up behind it; The fox's body is now a dense smooth mesh (about four times the rings and segments) with shoulder blades, elbows, round haunches and a loin hollow sculpted in, shaded with true surface normals, so it no longer reads as faceted low-poly; Hesper's crust now lands a little way round the keep floor from the fox instead of across toward the fire, so the fox bows to it and eats on open floor rather than with its head inside the lid's plinth stones, and its coat is a less saturated ginger with a darker saddle and broad lighter/darker patches; Before the fox gets up for Hesper's crust it now looks at where it fell, glances up round at her, and looks back before creeping to it; you hear each bite crunch through the dry crust as it eats (a crisp snap and a dry crackle of crumbs, quiet, only close by), and when it is gone it licks its chops twice, muzzle bobbing, and gives its head a quick side-to-side shake before going back to its seat; The fox at Hesper's fire now sits on swept open floor just in from the breach, swung off her way in, so its whole sat body, legs and brush show instead of hiding behind rubble slabs; Once a night, while Hesper sits up warming her hands and the fox sits at the edge of her firelight, she tosses it a heel of dark bread: her arm comes up with the crust by her ear and flicks out, the crust arcs over the floor and lands short of the fox; it gets up, creeps to it, bows its neck nose to the floor, sniffs, eats it with its head working, and goes back to its seat; she has a new warm line about it ("I keep the crusts back for it. Don't tell the deer."); At night you now hear the fox from the dark grass: every few minutes a run of hoarse high yaps from round its den, and one sharp yelp as it bolts from the hero; The fox at Hesper's fire now reads as a red fox from the front instead of a white bust: only a narrow cream strip down the throat, a red-buff belly, a longer narrower muzzle darkening to a black nose, bigger eyes that throw back a green-gold shine, buff ear insides and a fuller brush; When Hesper sits down for the night at half past nine she no longer drops straight into her doze: while the fire still blazes she sits up turned round to it, face lifted, both hands held out over her knees to the warmth, and talks to you awake ("Sit, if you like. The fire does not mind who it warms."); as it burns down after eleven she nods off, head to her knees and back square to the keep, and only mumbles; Hesper's firelight is a little less saturated so the wall at 1 am reads warm stone instead of salmon-red; A red fox now comes to Hesper's fire after dark: once the fire has burned a while (~21-22 h) it trots in out of the dark meadow through the curtain gateway, creeps the last metres and sits in the keep's breach at the edge of the firelight, brush curled round its paws, watching the flames and glancing round into the dark, eyes shining back the fire; it slips away before first light, when the fire burns low or rain comes on, and bolts back into the dark if the hero walks up on it, keeping away a couple of minutes after; The wall grass right behind Hesper's fire is now burnt off and the tufts a little further out singed dark, so no pale straw flares gold against the sooted stone at night; The moonlit meadow at night is silver-blue again: the firelight colour exemption in the night grade had let straw-dry grass go olive-yellow, now only orange (firelit) pixels keep their colour; Hesper's low fire now lights a smaller circle that dies off within a few metres; grass tufts no longer glow through with the fire's light from behind; At night firelight now keeps its colour: Hesper's fire lights the keep's inner face in warm amber stone instead of the night grade turning it lavender-grey, and the stones show their relief in it; Fireflies now keep to the open grass instead of drifting into the walled keep, and never sit on the lens as a big glowing blob; Hesper's low fire at 1 am glows a little stronger on the stones round it; The cold fire ring in the keep no longer glows cream at noon: its stones, logs and ash now sit dark and sooted in the shade; The soot over Hesper's fire now spreads wide enough to reach the keep wall behind it, so the courses above the flames go dark at night and at noon; The keep wall behind Hesper's fire no longer burns out to flat orange at night: the firelight falls off more gently, so the stones right behind the flames keep their relief, joints and ivy while the rest of the keep still glows; The keep stones over and round Hesper's fire are now smoke-blackened: a soot plume climbs the inner face behind the fire, widening as it rises, in billowed clouds with no moss or lichen in it, and the stones nearest the ring are scorched dark; Hesper's firelight is now a warmer amber instead of a saturated orange gel, hangs a little lower and glints less off the stone; The stones and ash round Hesper's fire no longer flare cream-white in its light: the flame's light now hangs a metre up over the logs and the ash bed is a darker grey, and the kindle view now looks from her side of the ring; The stones round Hesper's fire now lie as flat, sooted fieldstones instead of round puffy lumps; Hesper's fire can now be seen from across the ring, the inner face behind it glowing warm with the floor out to there swept; The floor round Hesper's cookfire is now swept: no spalls or loose slabs on the ash ring, where she kneels or on the open floor between, and a low glow off the embers lights her front, knees and hands as she kneels at the tinder; Each strike of Hesper's flint now clicks and rasps off the steel, and when the tinder takes you hear her blow into it twice and the low whump of the flame catching; she sets her willow basket down beside her bed before she kneels at the ring, so it no longer rides up over her bent back; Hesper now lights her cookfire herself at dusk: at half past seven she comes in from the breach, kneels at the ring facing it and strikes flint into the tinder -- five bursts of sparks -- until it catches; the fire smoulders and smokes, the first flames creep up, and she stays down feeding it until they take, then straightens with a hand to the small of her back and stands over it; if she is kept away (rain) it catches by the clock later; Hesper now keeps a cookfire on the keep floor beside her bed: a ring of sooted fieldstones round an ash bed with four charred oak logs in a star; she lights it as the light goes, it blazes through the evening with licking flames, rising sparks, smoke leaning off on the wind, a flickering warm light that throws shadows round the keep walls and a crackle and pop of burning wood, burns down to a low fire while she dozes and to embers by dawn, cold ash by noon; moths come in out of the dark to circle and dart at it, and rain knocks the flames down into hissing smoke; she has new lines about it; when Hesper crouches, the side gores of her robe now swing and fold with the front and back panels -- over the knees and down to the litter -- so no gap opens at her hips; when Hesper stoops to the litter her robe now hangs to gravity instead of tipping with her pelvis: the front panel lies along her thighs and falls over her knees to the leaves, and the back panel folds where it meets the ground and lies on the litter behind her instead of sticking out stiffly; the ferns on the grove floor no longer glow as bright fans when seen against the sun: a fern frond now scatters far less light forward than a thin oak leaf; the ferns on the grove floor are a deeper, cooler green with their own weaker back-light tint instead of the oak leaves' yellow-lime glow; Hesper's crouched body no longer shows the hero's black lacquer plates and iron knee caps and bracers: under her robe it reads as layered grey wool with linen bindings, and her slate robe is a shade lighter; the low side shot of Hesper picking in the litter is now aimed level with no grass blades in the lens; the leaf drifts under the gather oaks now read russet and warm tan over a brown leaf mould instead of greyish-pale leaves on near-black gaps; the leaf litter under the gather oaks now lies in drifts of overlapping leaves, six deep, thinning to a few scattered leaves on dark crumbled leaf mould between them, instead of an even polka-dot mosaic of pale separate leaves; the gather oaks' boles no longer stand as glossy black-green columns in back light: their bark reads grey-brown and plated with olive moss on the shaded side, and the crack rims no longer catch the sky as bright lines; the leaf litter under the oaks no longer glitters with cream-white flecks and pale pebbles: its leaves come in mixed sizes, russet, tan and wet brown, each with a soft shadow on the leaves below, and damp runs darken the bed; Hesper's robe is now woad washed out to slate grey instead of navy; Hesper now stands square-shouldered instead of with the hero's chest turned to one side, so the willow basket rides centred on her spine and the toss lands in its mouth; Under the oaks each find now goes in her basket: she comes up from the litter with a short crooked stick in her right hand, then reaches up over her right shoulder and drops it into the willow basket on her back before she stoops again; Under the oaks Hesper now gets right down to the litter -- knees folded, bent at the waist, her hand among the leaves -- and straightens with her right hand pressed to the small of her back; the deer know her: when she walks among them a grazing hind now lifts her head and watches her for a few seconds, then goes back to the grass, and they never bolt from her; Under the oaks Hesper now works the leaf litter: every few seconds she crouches, curls right down and reaches for an acorn or a stick, holds there, then straightens slowly with an old back, and she finishes each pick before moving on to the next oak; Hesper now has her own cut, so she no longer reads as a second hero: no straw hat, just her ash-grey hood, a faded robe to the ankles, a slight old stoop, and a woven willow basket of deadwood on her back, which she sets down beside her bed at night; Hesper now leaves the keep in the early afternoon and walks out into the open meadow below it, stopping here and there, 20-45 m out, to stand and look at the deer, who graze on round her, and she has new lines for her walk, for the rain ("The keep lost its roof before my grandmother was born.") and for the morning at the lid; The hero can now speak to Hesper: in reach a small brass keycap prompt reads "E Speak", and each word gets a subtitled line -- the first time she introduces herself and the sealed lid in three lines, after that a line that fits what she is doing and the hour (dawn watch, tending, gathering, eating, dusk watch, the ember at night, sheltering from rain), dry and a little sad; she stops and faces him while she talks, and dozing she only mumbles; Hesper, the last keeper of the Shrine of Binding, now lives in the ruined keep: she wakes against the inner wall at dawn and walks out through the breach to watch the light come up, tends the sealed lid through the morning, goes out through the curtain gateway to gather under the oaks, eats in the shade at noon, watches the vale from the breach at dusk, tends the lid again as the ember glows and sits down against the inner wall to doze, head on her knees, until dawn; under a rain front she hurries under the nearest oak crown until it passes; when the hero walks up she stops, turns to face him and the screen names her; The pause panel now has a Picture section: a Low/Medium/High/Ultra quality preset that scales GI, screen-space passes, shadow maps and the fog grid, and a render-scale slider that upscales with FSR 2, both kept between runs; Esc pauses the vale and opens a small lacquer-and-brass panel with sliders for master, ambience, wildlife, effects and music, kept between runs; inside the ruined keep the footfalls on the grit, the staff's swings and strikes ring off the stone in a short tight reverb while the wind and birds outside drop and dull behind the walls, fading back to open air as the hero walks out; the red deer now have voices: a hind barks a gruff alarm as the herd bolts and others take it up as they run, their hooves drum the turf in four-beat bounds (slapping and splashing on soaked ground), and at dusk and before dawn the stag bellows a long groaning rut roar down the vale; under a storm front the far mountains now sink to dark storm-grey silhouettes rising out of a low rain band instead of glowing as pale flat cut-outs; the far massifs' ribs and snow couloirs now wander, fork and change width from face to face, with fuller snowfields on the summits, instead of standing in even organ-pipe flutes; at night the far mountain ring now stands as layered moonlit blue-grey massifs with faint snow tops in the haze instead of a pure black cut-out, and at dusk it no longer glows pale over the darkened valley; the far massifs' mid and lower flanks now break into dark dwarf-pine ribs, pale grass couloirs, cliff bands with scree aprons and avalanche chutes through the forest, instead of one flat grey band; the far massifs now break into dark rock ribs with pale snow lying in the couloirs between them above the treeline, and dark forest climbs their lower flanks in tongues, instead of flat grey faces; the far mountains now stand as darker blue-grey massifs in layered haze instead of a milky wash; the practice post's iron band now reads as rusty forged iron -- brown oxide with orange bloom, hammer facets worn back to dull grey where the staff strikes, a crowned strap with rounded edges, two rivet heads on the overlap and rust runs staining the oak below -- instead of a flat black stripe; the far mountain ring now rises as broad shouldered massifs with long spurs, dark rock, alpine turf and forest climbing their flanks and snow only on the high tops, fading back in hazy layers, instead of a row of pale needle cones; the practice post's oak now reads as silver-grey sun-bleached wood with rain streaks instead of a black stump in back light, its cut top shows pale end grain with growth rings and radial checks, and its moss is a dark olive only at the damp foot instead of lime blotches on the cap; the straw practice post is now a bundle of hundreds of loose gold, grey and rust-damp stalks lashed with sagging twisted-rope rings tied off in knots with hanging tails, its cut ends splaying out like a broom, instead of a smooth striped cylinder; the hero and the Hollow no longer stand as black cut-outs with the sun behind them: the meadow throws warm light up into their shaded side and the sun wraps a faint rim round their silhouettes, so the red robe, ochre sash, linen wraps and the Hollow's grey ash skin read in back light; the fight and the body now have sound: the staff whips through each swing and whooms on the heavy, strikes thud into the straw post and crack off the Hollow with a crumble of ash and grit, a parry rings like struck iron, the smash thumps the ground and throws clods, a dodge whooshes, a perfect dodge swells and the whole mix slows and deepens in the slow motion, the Hollow's spear hisses through its thrust, and footfalls crunch in the grass, splash on soaked ground and scuff on the keep's gritty floor; the vale now has sound: a wind bed that swells with the weather, leaf rustle, rain hiss and patter, a dawn chorus and evening song from the oak groves, crickets in the night grass, rook caws and wing clatter when a flock bursts up, and thunder after every lightning stroke; the night meadow is now moonlit -- silver grass tops and faces over deep blue shadow -- instead of one flat grey-blue wash; at night the far mountain ring now sinks into faint moonlit silhouettes in the haze instead of standing as pale grey cut-outs against the black sky; fireflies at night now give the grass round them a soft warm-green glow instead of a hot lime pool; the hero's hood now wraps round the sides of his face cloth instead of standing as a thin strip beside it; the hero's hood now ends in a short collar over the shoulders instead of a long tongue hanging down his side; the hero's face cloth now tucks in behind the hood's front edge instead of hanging a point below it; the hero's hood now ends in a rounded edge that sweeps up under the chin instead of hanging two lumpy lappet points over the chest; the hero's eye whites now darken into the lids' shadow toward both corners with a moist pink-brown inner corner, the edges of his face sink into the hood's shadow, and at night his face keeps a little warmth instead of turning grey-lavender; the hero's eyes now sit under relaxed upper lids that cover the top of the iris, with greyer shaded whites, and his face skin carries a weathered grain with crow's feet, creases under the lower lids and two frown lines between the brows; the hero's hood and face cloth now read as heavy fulled wool -- fibre fuzz, pills, soft creases and a sheen at grazing angles -- the face cloth hangs in folds from the nose and tucks into the mantle collar so no pale armour shows, and the eyes carry a soft glint instead of a hot white dot; the hero's eyes now sit recessed under the lids as dark-irised almonds instead of bulging grey goggles, the face reads as sun-browned skin in the hat's shade instead of a bright orange doll, and the face cloth wraps down over the throat; the hero's hood now frames the face in a rounded arch with a thick rolled hem, drawn a little forward round the brow, instead of a straight-edged polygon cut; the light under the hero's hat now follows the time of day: a full warm fill at noon, dimmer and goldening through the evening, a dim cool moonlit glow at night instead of an orange lamp on the face; the curtain wall's stones now wear a dark grey weathering skin with more lichen and moss, their arrises and tops weathered dark instead of pale-edged; grass now sprouts from the low bed joints of the keep faces and the curtain wall, leaning out over the stone; the curtain's upper stones sit visibly out of true over a deeper talus; the ruined curtain wall round the keep now breaks down in stepped columns, its two stone faces robbed to different heights with stones fallen out of them and a grassy talus at both feet; the broken crown of the keep and curtain wall now tumbles -- a quarter of the top stones gone, others slid half off their beds and tipped over the face, loose stones lying askew on the courses, and grass tufts rooted in the open tops and the rubble core, the exposed bed tops weathered dark and mossy instead of pale; the keep stones now come from different beds of the quarry -- warm buff, ochre, rust-stained, pale and dark grey, with iron stains blooming from the joints -- and the joints sink into shadow and grime, with soil and moss lodged in the low ones; the ruined keep and its curtain wall are now laid in mixed stones -- long stretchers, short closers and headers bonding into the core -- on courses of varying height with the odd thin levelling course, open 2-6 cm joints and stones a little proud or sunk and out of true, with deeper chipped arrises, instead of brick-regular rows of identical blocks; the keep ivy now spreads sideways into branching mats instead of standing as vertical strands, with dark bronze-green young tips and dull yellowing leaves instead of white-looking specks; the keep ivy now lies pressed to the stone on short petioles with darker old leaves, a few yellowing and cold-bronzed ones among the green, and grows on the outer face either side of the breach where the approach looks at it; ivy now climbs the ruined keep: woody stems wander up the inner and outer faces from the wall foot, throw side shoots and carry dense shingled mats of dark glossy lobed leaves with pale veins and light young tips, thickest on the shaded north side and behind the shrine lid; the keep floor now carries oak leaves of three sizes heaped in drifts and windrows and dark fallen twigs, with no white pebble specks or stray daisies; the keep floor round the shrine plinth is now beaten earth paled by mortar dust, with gravel and stone chips, spalls knocked off the crown lying half sunk along the wall foot, oak leaves drifted against the walls and short grass tufts at the wall foot, instead of a flat dark texture; the oak plate tops now carry fibrous grain and raised flakes that break up the sun on the bark, and the cross cracks roll over into the split instead of ending at a hard edge; the keep of the watchtower ruin now holds the sealed way down to the Shrine of Binding: a carved greystone lid on a worn two-step plinth, its sigil of eight twisted binding cords glowing faint ember in the grooves and waking brighter, with pulses running in along the cords, as the hero walks up; standing on it names the place on screen -- the first shrine hook of the slice; oak bark now reads warm grey-brown in sun instead of birch-white, its cross cracks vary in spacing and width and run across several plates, and the moss cushions at the bole feet read as soft olive mounds instead of a crackled-mud net; oak bark now breaks into blocky plates cut across by cracks every 10-20 cm, with dark cavities in the furrows and cracks; oak boles now have real bark relief -- grey-brown plates pushed out and warm furrows sunk in, breaking the trunk silhouette -- with the moss climbing them in streaks and pale lichen on the drier bark; every oak now spreads 4-7 surface roots from its root flare that arch through the moss collar, worn bare on top and mossy where they sink into the soil, some forking into thinner side roots; the moss collars at the oak boles and boulder feet now read as olive cushions with dark peaty crevices and a few dried-brown tops instead of a flat lime disc, and the grove floor between them greens with patches of low moss instead of reading as bare red-brown soil from 15 m; boulder tops no longer show marble-like crack veining; the tall meadow grass and short sward no longer grow through the moss cushions at the oak boles and boulder feet -- the moss lies open, with a few tufts poking through its ragged edge; moss now creeps up the boulder flanks from their foot collars in tongues, higher on the shaded side, and climbs the shaded side of the oak boles in streaks up the furrows; every oak bole and boulder foot now sits in a ragged collar of velvet moss, wider on the shaded north side, with a few fallen oak leaves lying on it; the oak crowns are now masses of small lobed oak leaves in sprays, each leaf catching the sun at its own angle, with a darker, shaded inner crown, instead of a few hundred plate-sized painted leaves; the red deer herd now beds down in the meadow at night -- kneeling on the forelegs, then sinking onto the haunches, each deer in its own patch of pressed grass, chewing the cud or dozing -- springs up haunches-first to stare at a hero who comes into sight, lies down again once he has gone and is up grazing again before dawn; red deer now have a deep chest, a flank that tucks up into a hanging thigh and a shoulder blade and haunch that show under the coat; a red deer stag with a royal head of antlers and a dark shaggy rut mane now grazes at the edge of the hinds and gallops with them when they flee; a fleeing deer herd now combs a lane of laid-over grass through the meadow where it ran, standing up again over ~30 s, and hoof and boot prints show a rim of paler torn-up sod round a darker pressed floor; red deer now press cloven hoof prints into the meadow: paired slots where they walk, splayed four-hoof groups with dew claws where they galloped, fading over ~2.5 min; the hero now has a face: sun-browned skin, brows and two wet eyes looking out between the hood and the face cloth under the hat brim, lit by a soft face fill; the hero's mantle now falls in deep uneven folds to a soft scalloped, stitched hem instead of a flat slab; the hero's old sci-fi plates now read as worn black lacquered lamellar with iron fittings and a dark indigo face cloth instead of a blue visor; the hero now travels in a wide woven straw hat over an indigo hood, a dark shoulder mantle, long madder-red robe panels front and back that swing with his stride, an ochre sash and linen leg and forearm bindings over the old armour; the tall meadow is now a mix of leafy clumps, low arching tussocks and seeding stems with straw panicles growing in knots and open runs; a dense short sward now carpets the meadow floor round the hero between the tall grass clumps, so the near ground reads as grass, not tufts over bare soil; a herd of red deer hinds and calves grazes the open meadow, heads up to stare at a hero who comes into sight and bolting together in a gallop when he runs at them, settling to graze far off; the hero and the Hollow soldier press boot prints into the ground that fill with rain and fade over ~3 min, and the trodden track through the meadow reads as a lane of laid, paler straw; the meadow grass, wildflowers and ferns part round the hero and stay trodden down along the trail, springing back over ~9 s; the soldier fight can now be lost and retried from the rest point; walkable test valley under a drifting cloud sky whose cumulus throw moving shadows across the meadow and the far ridges, with weather fronts that roll in (cumulus thicken into a grey deck, wind, grey light, rain, wet ground, puddles that fill the hollows and dance with raindrop rings, drops that splash on the ground, a hero and soldier who get soaked, drip and dry off again, and pools the hero splashes through, leaving rings spreading behind each step; heavy fronts throw lightning in the far deck that flashes the clouds and the land), grass, oak groves with a fern and shrub understory over a carpet of fallen oak leaves, boulders, a ruined watchtower to climb up to and explore on the western spur, rook flocks, wildflower drifts, butterflies by day, fireflies at night and midges in the evening light, played as a rigged animated humanoid Harrowmere's first house now stands on the west edge of the green, its door toward where the hero wakes: a timber-framed cottage on a dry-stone footing, hewn oak frame with limewashed wattle-and-daub panels, a plank door with iron straps, a mullioned window, a steep roof of ~1900 individually laid riven slates with lichen, a stone ridge and a stone chimney up the west gable; you cannot walk through it and the deer steer round it; When the fox sits, its brush (now 0.5 m long) lies on the turf out of the rump and curls round its right haunch with the tip beside its forepaws, instead of sweeping up behind it; The fox's body is now a dense smooth mesh (about four times the rings and segments) with shoulder blades, elbows, round haunches and a loin hollow sculpted in, shaded with true surface normals, so it no longer reads as faceted low-poly; Hesper's crust now lands a little way round the keep floor from the fox instead of across toward the fire, so the fox bows to it and eats on open floor rather than with its head inside the lid's plinth stones, and its coat is a less saturated ginger with a darker saddle and broad lighter/darker patches; Before the fox gets up for Hesper's crust it now looks at where it fell, glances up round at her, and looks back before creeping to it; you hear each bite crunch through the dry crust as it eats (a crisp snap and a dry crackle of crumbs, quiet, only close by), and when it is gone it licks its chops twice, muzzle bobbing, and gives its head a quick side-to-side shake before going back to its seat; The fox at Hesper's fire now sits on swept open floor just in from the breach, swung off her way in, so its whole sat body, legs and brush show instead of hiding behind rubble slabs; Once a night, while Hesper sits up warming her hands and the fox sits at the edge of her firelight, she tosses it a heel of dark bread: her arm comes up with the crust by her ear and flicks out, the crust arcs over the floor and lands short of the fox; it gets up, creeps to it, bows its neck nose to the floor, sniffs, eats it with its head working, and goes back to its seat; she has a new warm line about it ("I keep the crusts back for it. Don't tell the deer."); At night you now hear the fox from the dark grass: every few minutes a run of hoarse high yaps from round its den, and one sharp yelp as it bolts from the hero; The fox at Hesper's fire now reads as a red fox from the front instead of a white bust: only a narrow cream strip down the throat, a red-buff belly, a longer narrower muzzle darkening to a black nose, bigger eyes that throw back a green-gold shine, buff ear insides and a fuller brush; When Hesper sits down for the night at half past nine she no longer drops straight into her doze: while the fire still blazes she sits up turned round to it, face lifted, both hands held out over her knees to the warmth, and talks to you awake ("Sit, if you like. The fire does not mind who it warms."); as it burns down after eleven she nods off, head to her knees and back square to the keep, and only mumbles; Hesper's firelight is a little less saturated so the wall at 1 am reads warm stone instead of salmon-red; A red fox now comes to Hesper's fire after dark: once the fire has burned a while (~21-22 h) it trots in out of the dark meadow through the curtain gateway, creeps the last metres and sits in the keep's breach at the edge of the firelight, brush curled round its paws, watching the flames and glancing round into the dark, eyes shining back the fire; it slips away before first light, when the fire burns low or rain comes on, and bolts back into the dark if the hero walks up on it, keeping away a couple of minutes after; The wall grass right behind Hesper's fire is now burnt off and the tufts a little further out singed dark, so no pale straw flares gold against the sooted stone at night; The moonlit meadow at night is silver-blue again: the firelight colour exemption in the night grade had let straw-dry grass go olive-yellow, now only orange (firelit) pixels keep their colour; Hesper's low fire now lights a smaller circle that dies off within a few metres; grass tufts no longer glow through with the fire's light from behind; At night firelight now keeps its colour: Hesper's fire lights the keep's inner face in warm amber stone instead of the night grade turning it lavender-grey, and the stones show their relief in it; Fireflies now keep to the open grass instead of drifting into the walled keep, and never sit on the lens as a big glowing blob; Hesper's low fire at 1 am glows a little stronger on the stones round it; The cold fire ring in the keep no longer glows cream at noon: its stones, logs and ash now sit dark and sooted in the shade; The soot over Hesper's fire now spreads wide enough to reach the keep wall behind it, so the courses above the flames go dark at night and at noon; The keep wall behind Hesper's fire no longer burns out to flat orange at night: the firelight falls off more gently, so the stones right behind the flames keep their relief, joints and ivy while the rest of the keep still glows; The keep stones over and round Hesper's fire are now smoke-blackened: a soot plume climbs the inner face behind the fire, widening as it rises, in billowed clouds with no moss or lichen in it, and the stones nearest the ring are scorched dark; Hesper's firelight is now a warmer amber instead of a saturated orange gel, hangs a little lower and glints less off the stone; The stones and ash round Hesper's fire no longer flare cream-white in its light: the flame's light now hangs a metre up over the logs and the ash bed is a darker grey, and the kindle view now looks from her side of the ring; The stones round Hesper's fire now lie as flat, sooted fieldstones instead of round puffy lumps; Hesper's fire can now be seen from across the ring, the inner face behind it glowing warm with the floor out to there swept; The floor round Hesper's cookfire is now swept: no spalls or loose slabs on the ash ring, where she kneels or on the open floor between, and a low glow off the embers lights her front, knees and hands as she kneels at the tinder; Each strike of Hesper's flint now clicks and rasps off the steel, and when the tinder takes you hear her blow into it twice and the low whump of the flame catching; she sets her willow basket down beside her bed before she kneels at the ring, so it no longer rides up over her bent back; Hesper now lights her cookfire herself at dusk: at half past seven she comes in from the breach, kneels at the ring facing it and strikes flint into the tinder -- five bursts of sparks -- until it catches; the fire smoulders and smokes, the first flames creep up, and she stays down feeding it until they take, then straightens with a hand to the small of her back and stands over it; if she is kept away (rain) it catches by the clock later; Hesper now keeps a cookfire on the keep floor beside her bed: a ring of sooted fieldstones round an ash bed with four charred oak logs in a star; she lights it as the light goes, it blazes through the evening with licking flames, rising sparks, smoke leaning off on the wind, a flickering warm light that throws shadows round the keep walls and a crackle and pop of burning wood, burns down to a low fire while she dozes and to embers by dawn, cold ash by noon; moths come in out of the dark to circle and dart at it, and rain knocks the flames down into hissing smoke; she has new lines about it; when Hesper crouches, the side gores of her robe now swing and fold with the front and back panels -- over the knees and down to the litter -- so no gap opens at her hips; when Hesper stoops to the litter her robe now hangs to gravity instead of tipping with her pelvis: the front panel lies along her thighs and falls over her knees to the leaves, and the back panel folds where it meets the ground and lies on the litter behind her instead of sticking out stiffly; the ferns on the grove floor no longer glow as bright fans when seen against the sun: a fern frond now scatters far less light forward than a thin oak leaf; the ferns on the grove floor are a deeper, cooler green with their own weaker back-light tint instead of the oak leaves' yellow-lime glow; Hesper's crouched body no longer shows the hero's black lacquer plates and iron knee caps and bracers: under her robe it reads as layered grey wool with linen bindings, and her slate robe is a shade lighter; the low side shot of Hesper picking in the litter is now aimed level with no grass blades in the lens; the leaf drifts under the gather oaks now read russet and warm tan over a brown leaf mould instead of greyish-pale leaves on near-black gaps; the leaf litter under the gather oaks now lies in drifts of overlapping leaves, six deep, thinning to a few scattered leaves on dark crumbled leaf mould between them, instead of an even polka-dot mosaic of pale separate leaves; the gather oaks' boles no longer stand as glossy black-green columns in back light: their bark reads grey-brown and plated with olive moss on the shaded side, and the crack rims no longer catch the sky as bright lines; the leaf litter under the oaks no longer glitters with cream-white flecks and pale pebbles: its leaves come in mixed sizes, russet, tan and wet brown, each with a soft shadow on the leaves below, and damp runs darken the bed; Hesper's robe is now woad washed out to slate grey instead of navy; Hesper now stands square-shouldered instead of with the hero's chest turned to one side, so the willow basket rides centred on her spine and the toss lands in its mouth; Under the oaks each find now goes in her basket: she comes up from the litter with a short crooked stick in her right hand, then reaches up over her right shoulder and drops it into the willow basket on her back before she stoops again; Under the oaks Hesper now gets right down to the litter -- knees folded, bent at the waist, her hand among the leaves -- and straightens with her right hand pressed to the small of her back; the deer know her: when she walks among them a grazing hind now lifts her head and watches her for a few seconds, then goes back to the grass, and they never bolt from her; Under the oaks Hesper now works the leaf litter: every few seconds she crouches, curls right down and reaches for an acorn or a stick, holds there, then straightens slowly with an old back, and she finishes each pick before moving on to the next oak; Hesper now has her own cut, so she no longer reads as a second hero: no straw hat, just her ash-grey hood, a faded robe to the ankles, a slight old stoop, and a woven willow basket of deadwood on her back, which she sets down beside her bed at night; Hesper now leaves the keep in the early afternoon and walks out into the open meadow below it, stopping here and there, 20-45 m out, to stand and look at the deer, who graze on round her, and she has new lines for her walk, for the rain ("The keep lost its roof before my grandmother was born.") and for the morning at the lid; The hero can now speak to Hesper: in reach a small brass keycap prompt reads "E Speak", and each word gets a subtitled line -- the first time she introduces herself and the sealed lid in three lines, after that a line that fits what she is doing and the hour (dawn watch, tending, gathering, eating, dusk watch, the ember at night, sheltering from rain), dry and a little sad; she stops and faces him while she talks, and dozing she only mumbles; Hesper, the last keeper of the Shrine of Binding, now lives in the ruined keep: she wakes against the inner wall at dawn and walks out through the breach to watch the light come up, tends the sealed lid through the morning, goes out through the curtain gateway to gather under the oaks, eats in the shade at noon, watches the vale from the breach at dusk, tends the lid again as the ember glows and sits down against the inner wall to doze, head on her knees, until dawn; under a rain front she hurries under the nearest oak crown until it passes; when the hero walks up she stops, turns to face him and the screen names her; The pause panel now has a Picture section: a Low/Medium/High/Ultra quality preset that scales GI, screen-space passes, shadow maps and the fog grid, and a render-scale slider that upscales with FSR 2, both kept between runs; Esc pauses the vale and opens a small lacquer-and-brass panel with sliders for master, ambience, wildlife, effects and music, kept between runs; inside the ruined keep the footfalls on the grit, the staff's swings and strikes ring off the stone in a short tight reverb while the wind and birds outside drop and dull behind the walls, fading back to open air as the hero walks out; the red deer now have voices: a hind barks a gruff alarm as the herd bolts and others take it up as they run, their hooves drum the turf in four-beat bounds (slapping and splashing on soaked ground), and at dusk and before dawn the stag bellows a long groaning rut roar down the vale; under a storm front the far mountains now sink to dark storm-grey silhouettes rising out of a low rain band instead of glowing as pale flat cut-outs; the far massifs' ribs and snow couloirs now wander, fork and change width from face to face, with fuller snowfields on the summits, instead of standing in even organ-pipe flutes; at night the far mountain ring now stands as layered moonlit blue-grey massifs with faint snow tops in the haze instead of a pure black cut-out, and at dusk it no longer glows pale over the darkened valley; the far massifs' mid and lower flanks now break into dark dwarf-pine ribs, pale grass couloirs, cliff bands with scree aprons and avalanche chutes through the forest, instead of one flat grey band; the far massifs now break into dark rock ribs with pale snow lying in the couloirs between them above the treeline, and dark forest climbs their lower flanks in tongues, instead of flat grey faces; the far mountains now stand as darker blue-grey massifs in layered haze instead of a milky wash; the practice post's iron band now reads as rusty forged iron -- brown oxide with orange bloom, hammer facets worn back to dull grey where the staff strikes, a crowned strap with rounded edges, two rivet heads on the overlap and rust runs staining the oak below -- instead of a flat black stripe; the far mountain ring now rises as broad shouldered massifs with long spurs, dark rock, alpine turf and forest climbing their flanks and snow only on the high tops, fading back in hazy layers, instead of a row of pale needle cones; the practice post's oak now reads as silver-grey sun-bleached wood with rain streaks instead of a black stump in back light, its cut top shows pale end grain with growth rings and radial checks, and its moss is a dark olive only at the damp foot instead of lime blotches on the cap; the straw practice post is now a bundle of hundreds of loose gold, grey and rust-damp stalks lashed with sagging twisted-rope rings tied off in knots with hanging tails, its cut ends splaying out like a broom, instead of a smooth striped cylinder; the hero and the Hollow no longer stand as black cut-outs with the sun behind them: the meadow throws warm light up into their shaded side and the sun wraps a faint rim round their silhouettes, so the red robe, ochre sash, linen wraps and the Hollow's grey ash skin read in back light; the fight and the body now have sound: the staff whips through each swing and whooms on the heavy, strikes thud into the straw post and crack off the Hollow with a crumble of ash and grit, a parry rings like struck iron, the smash thumps the ground and throws clods, a dodge whooshes, a perfect dodge swells and the whole mix slows and deepens in the slow motion, the Hollow's spear hisses through its thrust, and footfalls crunch in the grass, splash on soaked ground and scuff on the keep's gritty floor; the vale now has sound: a wind bed that swells with the weather, leaf rustle, rain hiss and patter, a dawn chorus and evening song from the oak groves, crickets in the night grass, rook caws and wing clatter when a flock bursts up, and thunder after every lightning stroke; the night meadow is now moonlit -- silver grass tops and faces over deep blue shadow -- instead of one flat grey-blue wash; at night the far mountain ring now sinks into faint moonlit silhouettes in the haze instead of standing as pale grey cut-outs against the black sky; fireflies at night now give the grass round them a soft warm-green glow instead of a hot lime pool; the hero's hood now wraps round the sides of his face cloth instead of standing as a thin strip beside it; the hero's hood now ends in a short collar over the shoulders instead of a long tongue hanging down his side; the hero's face cloth now tucks in behind the hood's front edge instead of hanging a point below it; the hero's hood now ends in a rounded edge that sweeps up under the chin instead of hanging two lumpy lappet points over the chest; the hero's eye whites now darken into the lids' shadow toward both corners with a moist pink-brown inner corner, the edges of his face sink into the hood's shadow, and at night his face keeps a little warmth instead of turning grey-lavender; the hero's eyes now sit under relaxed upper lids that cover the top of the iris, with greyer shaded whites, and his face skin carries a weathered grain with crow's feet, creases under the lower lids and two frown lines between the brows; the hero's hood and face cloth now read as heavy fulled wool -- fibre fuzz, pills, soft creases and a sheen at grazing angles -- the face cloth hangs in folds from the nose and tucks into the mantle collar so no pale armour shows, and the eyes carry a soft glint instead of a hot white dot; the hero's eyes now sit recessed under the lids as dark-irised almonds instead of bulging grey goggles, the face reads as sun-browned skin in the hat's shade instead of a bright orange doll, and the face cloth wraps down over the throat; the hero's hood now frames the face in a rounded arch with a thick rolled hem, drawn a little forward round the brow, instead of a straight-edged polygon cut; the light under the hero's hat now follows the time of day: a full warm fill at noon, dimmer and goldening through the evening, a dim cool moonlit glow at night instead of an orange lamp on the face; the curtain wall's stones now wear a dark grey weathering skin with more lichen and moss, their arrises and tops weathered dark instead of pale-edged; grass now sprouts from the low bed joints of the keep faces and the curtain wall, leaning out over the stone; the curtain's upper stones sit visibly out of true over a deeper talus; the ruined curtain wall round the keep now breaks down in stepped columns, its two stone faces robbed to different heights with stones fallen out of them and a grassy talus at both feet; the broken crown of the keep and curtain wall now tumbles -- a quarter of the top stones gone, others slid half off their beds and tipped over the face, loose stones lying askew on the courses, and grass tufts rooted in the open tops and the rubble core, the exposed bed tops weathered dark and mossy instead of pale; the keep stones now come from different beds of the quarry -- warm buff, ochre, rust-stained, pale and dark grey, with iron stains blooming from the joints -- and the joints sink into shadow and grime, with soil and moss lodged in the low ones; the ruined keep and its curtain wall are now laid in mixed stones -- long stretchers, short closers and headers bonding into the core -- on courses of varying height with the odd thin levelling course, open 2-6 cm joints and stones a little proud or sunk and out of true, with deeper chipped arrises, instead of brick-regular rows of identical blocks; the keep ivy now spreads sideways into branching mats instead of standing as vertical strands, with dark bronze-green young tips and dull yellowing leaves instead of white-looking specks; the keep ivy now lies pressed to the stone on short petioles with darker old leaves, a few yellowing and cold-bronzed ones among the green, and grows on the outer face either side of the breach where the approach looks at it; ivy now climbs the ruined keep: woody stems wander up the inner and outer faces from the wall foot, throw side shoots and carry dense shingled mats of dark glossy lobed leaves with pale veins and light young tips, thickest on the shaded north side and behind the shrine lid; the keep floor now carries oak leaves of three sizes heaped in drifts and windrows and dark fallen twigs, with no white pebble specks or stray daisies; the keep floor round the shrine plinth is now beaten earth paled by mortar dust, with gravel and stone chips, spalls knocked off the crown lying half sunk along the wall foot, oak leaves drifted against the walls and short grass tufts at the wall foot, instead of a flat dark texture; the oak plate tops now carry fibrous grain and raised flakes that break up the sun on the bark, and the cross cracks roll over into the split instead of ending at a hard edge; the keep of the watchtower ruin now holds the sealed way down to the Shrine of Binding: a carved greystone lid on a worn two-step plinth, its sigil of eight twisted binding cords glowing faint ember in the grooves and waking brighter, with pulses running in along the cords, as the hero walks up; standing on it names the place on screen -- the first shrine hook of the slice; oak bark now reads warm grey-brown in sun instead of birch-white, its cross cracks vary in spacing and width and run across several plates, and the moss cushions at the bole feet read as soft olive mounds instead of a crackled-mud net; oak bark now breaks into blocky plates cut across by cracks every 10-20 cm, with dark cavities in the furrows and cracks; oak boles now have real bark relief -- grey-brown plates pushed out and warm furrows sunk in, breaking the trunk silhouette -- with the moss climbing them in streaks and pale lichen on the drier bark; every oak now spreads 4-7 surface roots from its root flare that arch through the moss collar, worn bare on top and mossy where they sink into the soil, some forking into thinner side roots; the moss collars at the oak boles and boulder feet now read as olive cushions with dark peaty crevices and a few dried-brown tops instead of a flat lime disc, and the grove floor between them greens with patches of low moss instead of reading as bare red-brown soil from 15 m; boulder tops no longer show marble-like crack veining; the tall meadow grass and short sward no longer grow through the moss cushions at the oak boles and boulder feet -- the moss lies open, with a few tufts poking through its ragged edge; moss now creeps up the boulder flanks from their foot collars in tongues, higher on the shaded side, and climbs the shaded side of the oak boles in streaks up the furrows; every oak bole and boulder foot now sits in a ragged collar of velvet moss, wider on the shaded north side, with a few fallen oak leaves lying on it; the oak crowns are now masses of small lobed oak leaves in sprays, each leaf catching the sun at its own angle, with a darker, shaded inner crown, instead of a few hundred plate-sized painted leaves; the red deer herd now beds down in the meadow at night -- kneeling on the forelegs, then sinking onto the haunches, each deer in its own patch of pressed grass, chewing the cud or dozing -- springs up haunches-first to stare at a hero who comes into sight, lies down again once he has gone and is up grazing again before dawn; red deer now have a deep chest, a flank that tucks up into a hanging thigh and a shoulder blade and haunch that show under the coat; a red deer stag with a royal head of antlers and a dark shaggy rut mane now grazes at the edge of the hinds and gallops with them when they flee; a fleeing deer herd now combs a lane of laid-over grass through the meadow where it ran, standing up again over ~30 s, and hoof and boot prints show a rim of paler torn-up sod round a darker pressed floor; red deer now press cloven hoof prints into the meadow: paired slots where they walk, splayed four-hoof groups with dew claws where they galloped, fading over ~2.5 min; the hero now has a face: sun-browned skin, brows and two wet eyes looking out between the hood and the face cloth under the hat brim, lit by a soft face fill; the hero's mantle now falls in deep uneven folds to a soft scalloped, stitched hem instead of a flat slab; the hero's old sci-fi plates now read as worn black lacquered lamellar with iron fittings and a dark indigo face cloth instead of a blue visor; the hero now travels in a wide woven straw hat over an indigo hood, a dark shoulder mantle, long madder-red robe panels front and back that swing with his stride, an ochre sash and linen leg and forearm bindings over the old armour; the tall meadow is now a mix of leafy clumps, low arching tussocks and seeding stems with straw panicles growing in knots and open runs; a dense short sward now carpets the meadow floor round the hero between the tall grass clumps, so the near ground reads as grass, not tufts over bare soil; a herd of red deer hinds and calves grazes the open meadow, heads up to stare at a hero who comes into sight and bolting together in a gallop when he runs at them, settling to graze far off; the hero and the Hollow soldier press boot prints into the ground that fill with rain and fade over ~3 min, and the trodden track through the meadow reads as a lane of laid, paler straw; the meadow grass, wildflowers and ferns part round the hero and stay trodden down along the trail, springing back over ~9 s; the soldier fight can now be lost and retried from the rest point; walkable test valley under a drifting cloud sky whose cumulus throw moving shadows across the meadow and the far ridges, with weather fronts that roll in (cumulus thicken into a grey deck, wind, grey light, rain, wet ground, puddles that fill the hollows and dance with raindrop rings, drops that splash on the ground, a hero and soldier who get soaked, drip and dry off again, and pools the hero splashes through, leaving rings spreading behind each step; heavy fronts throw lightning in the far deck that flashes the clouds and the land), grass, oak groves with a fern and shrub understory over a carpet of fallen oak leaves, boulders, a ruined watchtower to climb up to and explore on the western spur, rook flocks, wildflower drifts, butterflies by day, fireflies at night and midges in the evening light, played as a rigged animated humanoid
who can draw the staff, land a four-hit string and a chargeable heavy smash on a practice post, parry, and lock on to and fight one Hollow Soldier (thrust + sweep) down the meadow
(a few minutes of real combat); one short quest (Bread for the Keep), no shrines yet. Game scope is
now ~30 h per the owner's direction in CLAUDE.md.)
Current milestone: 1 (vertical slice, Harrowmere Vale). Milestone 0 is done: DESIGN.md written.

## Environment / renderer (cloud box)
- Godot 4.7.2 is fetched by `tools/cloud-setup.sh` into `.tools/godot` (gitignored).
  Run it first thing every session.
- Shots now take ~10 min each on the cloud box (more if bots run at the same time). The
  xvfb timeout is `SHOT_TIMEOUT` (default 3000 s since 09:00 10-15, was 1500 s): a Vulkan shot that timed out
  used to fall back to GL compatibility silently, giving a non-representative image (dark,
  no fog). If a shot log says "retrying opengl3", the frame is not a Forward+ frame. Do not
  run bots alongside a shot.
- **Forward+ (Vulkan) renders headless** under `xvfb-run` with Mesa lavapipe/llvmpipe
  (verified: "Vulkan 1.4 - Forward+ - llvmpipe"). SDFGI, SSAO, SSIL, volumetric fog, glow all
  run. A 1280x720 screenshot with 60 warm-up frames takes ~75 s of CPU; budget shots
  accordingly (3–5 per session, run them in the background while editing).
- `tools/shots.sh "<spot> <name> [frames] [hour]"` -> `shots/<name>.png`. Spots are defined in
  `game/tools/shot.gd` (village, hill, valley, ridge, player). Falls back to opengl3 if Vulkan
  fails. Uses Weston on the local box, xvfb on cloud.
- `tools/bots.sh [route...]` runs `game/tools/bot.gd` headless (routes: walk, ridge). Bots run at
  4x time scale; both pass in ~10 s wall each.
- Headless import: `.tools/godot --headless --path game --import` (REQUIRED once per fresh
  box BEFORE the first shot: without it shot.gd fails to parse, Vulkan "fails" and it
  falls back to opengl3 after ~10 min, saving nothing).
- (04:00 10-16) Do not lower `SHOT_TIMEOUT` below the 3000 s default: a 1500 s Vulkan
  `keeper` shot timed out and fell back to GL. To stop a shot, kill by PID from
  `ps -eo pid,comm | grep -E "godot|Xvfb"`; `pkill -f`/`pgrep -f` with a pattern also
  matches the tool's own shell command line and kills it (exit 144).

## Done
- (05:00 10-30) SECOND QUEST: *The Last of the Flock* (Osric). Quests.FLOCK: after his 2 intro lines Osric asks
  for the bell his bellwether Moss wore (lost up the fell the winter the keep went dark) -> the hero walks up the
  fell from his stile (~105 m, +27 m) to the great grey stone (Flora boulder at (-113.5, 101.3), size 3.1) where
  world/fell_bell.gd (`FellBell`, new) stands: a leaning silvered-oak fold post (timber.gdshader, split top,
  collision), a black cracked leather collar wrapped twice round it, and a lathe-turned cast-bronze sheep bell
  (beaded lip, canon loop, iron clapper; shaders/fell_bell.gdshader: patina, verdigris in the mouth and on the
  upper faces, lip worn back to warm bronze, wet darkening) on a two-strand drop. The bell swings as a damped
  pendulum pushed by Weather.wind gusts and knocks (synthesised inharmonic bronze partials, 3 pitch variants,
  heard to 70 m) at the hard turns of its swing, so you hear it before you see it. At quest step 1 the HUD offers

- (22:05 10-27) Night canopy band FIXED (Next 20:40 item 1). Root cause, from Godot's scene_forward_clustered.glsl:
  a shader that writes FOG (CUSTOM_FOG_USED) skips the volumetric fog entirely, so the moonlit volume hazed the sky
  (sky affect 1) and the wood line but never the horizon ring, whose dark silhouette cut a ruled edge across the hazed
  crowns. horizon.gdshader now re-applies the volume itself (vol_sun / vol_moon x Henyey-Greenstein phase + vol_emit,
  alpha 1-exp(-density x length), blended over its own FOG exactly as the engine blends volumetric over depth fog);
  world.gd _apply_horizon_haze feeds it from the environment + sun/moon. The physical estimate (gain 1, shot
  cotvolring) changed nothing visible: the engine's moonlit volume is far brighter than albedo x moon x phase
  predicts (cause unknown; SDFGI is off on llvmpipe). Calibrated by eye: RING_VOL_MOON_GAIN 18 (cotvolring2, gallery
  *-night-ring-haze-overbright.jpg) made flat pale cut-outs; 4 (cotvolring3, gallery *-night-ring-haze.jpg) puts the
  ring and wood in one haze, band gone, massifs a touch flat/pale. Sun term is ungained (day unverified, should be a
  small change: day density 0.0007). Bots walk ridge PASS.

  keep's knoll (Flora.ruin_site, core r 9 m) and the two best sun-facing crags 100-180 m out
  (height over a 20 m ring + 60 x how far the noon-sun-facing slope beats flat; >= 90 m apart, off
  the east road): found at (-150,-22) keep, (0,150), (150,80). lift_at(p): Gaussian core
  exp(-1.1 d^2) minus a sink ring 0.45 x exp(-((d-2.1)/0.6)^2), d over a radius that widens 60 % to
  the top (75 m), axis leaning downwind 0.12..0.6 m/m with the wind, faded in over the first 6 m and
  out over the top third; LIFT 3.6 m/s x strength. strength = sin((h-7.5)/11 pi)^0.8 x (1-0.8 cloud)

  _glide_tick: glide_lift adds to the sink target; the vertical now eases toward the target at 4
  m/s^2 when rising (30 still arrests the fall at open; the jump rise now stops at the target instead

- (18:00 10-23) **Robe follows the climb** (bot-verified `climb`; shot `climb4`, verdict FAIL, gallery *-climb-robe.jpg).

  over 0.2 s lost game time and the herd ran at ~60% pace against the physics-driven hero
  (anchor 25-30 m per 4 s instead of 46): cap raised to 15 steps (0.5 s); (2) the rim turn
  (probe past 200 m) always swung toward the world centre, which could turn the herd back
  across the hero's line; it now picks the tangent away from the threat, latched once per
  flight (`_rim_side`, reset in `_bolt`) so a flight straight at the rim can't dither. Herd
  bot PASS (68 m ahead, settles 111 m off). `DryWall`: head joints now vary 0.8-5 cm and
  60% of the wide ones get a small pinning chip; the hearting box uses a new

  time; dropped the duplicate. Two notes for whoever touches it next: colour literals in a
  shader are linear, so sRGB moss colours must be `source_color` uniforms (written as vec3
  literals they turn a boulder flat bright lime); the reach is measured above the sunk
  instance origin, not the local terrain, so boulders on slopes get more moss downhill.
  Sessions still overlap even after a pull at the start: pull again right before building.

- Session 2026-10-11 20:00 UTC:
  - **Oak crowns no longer blow out to white toward the sun (bug, next item 9 pick).** Cause:
    leaf `SPECULAR = 0.4` gives f0 = 0.16 * 0.4^2 = 0.026, and Godot's Fresnel peak
    f90 = clamp(f0 * 50) clamps to 1.0, so every sun-facing card hit full grazing sheen
    looking toward the sun. Now SPECULAR 0.2 (f90 ~0.3), roughness 0.72, backlight
    `col * (0.5, 0.6, 0.3)` (yellow-green transmission, weaker than the lit face).

    brown, the yellowing share more olive.

    tops, no blossom), `lf_ridge`/`lf_ridge2` (15 h near oak: apple look gone after the atlas
    tweak). 13/13 bots pass. Note: the first Vulkan shot on a fresh box needs ~8 min (shader
    cache) and the `timeout 600` in shots.sh can let it fall back to opengl3; run the import
    first and allow time.
- Session 2026-10-11 19:00 UTC:
  - **Cloud shadows on the land (next item 9 / bug "clouds cast no shadows").** New
    `shaders/cloud_shadow.gdshaderinc` holds the deck's density function, shared by the sky
    dome (`clouds.gdshader` now includes it, so the deck drawn in the sky and the shadows on
    the ground are the same clouds) and `cloud_shade_at(world_pos)`: projects the point

  - **Living world first pass: rook flocks and soaring kites (was Next 5, birds half).**
    New `world/birds.gd` + `shaders/bird.gdshader`, all procedural: one rook mesh (~0.45 m,
    0.85 m span: lofted body, head, pale beak, eye beads, fanned tail, wings with five
    fingered primaries, legs and toes), posed per instance through INSTANCE_CUSTOM (wing
    flap angle, leg tuck, head peck, wing fold). 3 flocks x 14 rooks feed on open meadow
    sites (sites rejected near trees/slopes): they hop, turn and peck. When the player or
    camera comes within 11 m (+1.4 m per m/s of horizontal speed) the flock bursts up, the
    nearest birds first, climbs on fast beats, wheels overhead with flap/glide bursts and
    banking for 8–13 s, then picks a clear site away from the threat and glides in with a
    flare (fast beats, legs down). Flocks also lift off unprompted every 70–160 s. Two red
    kites soar high circles by day (hidden at night). Bird sim clamps dt to 1/30 so slow
    screenshot frames don't teleport them.
  - Grass: `GrassField.cropped_spots` — turf is cropped short (22 %) around feeding sites
    so the rooks are visible instead of buried in 50 cm grass.
  - Bots: `walk` asserts the rooks scatter when the route crosses their site (PASS,
    4 scatters, 1 landing); `ridge` PASS. Shot spots `birds` (camera 9 m: take-off) and
    `rooks` (15.5 m: feeding).
  - Verdicts: `rooks` 14h — black rooks hopping on a cropped patch under the oak line;
    reads like a real field. Fail: birds tiny, crop patch shows the bare brown ground.
    `birds` 11h — rooks lifting out of the meadow; alive in motion, specks in a still. Fail.
- Session 2026-10-10 12:00 UTC:
  - **Far mountain ring: the world no longer ends at 256 m (was Next 3).** New
    `world/horizon.gd` + `shaders/horizon.gdshader`: one ArrayMesh on a non-uniform grid
    from the tile edge out to 2.8 km (2 m cells on the seam, sharing the terrain's edge
    vertices exactly; growing to 40 m cells), cells inside the tile skipped. Height follows
    `Terrain.height_at` at the seam, blends into rolling foothills, then a ridged mountain
    wall up to ~520 m with a lower notch to the south-east (the Greywold road). Shader:
    meadow at the seam, forest patches on foothills, strata rock on steep faces, snow above
    ~380 m. Visual only (no collision). ~246k tris, builds in ~1.5 s on the cloud CPU.
  - Verdicts: `village` 12h — hazy blue-grey ranges with snowy peaks close the horizon;
    first shot with real depth. Ridges look faceted at distance, peaks a uniform sawtooth.
    Fail (close). `hill` 16.5h — layered ranges good; foreground grass in deep shadow.
    `east` 17.5h — mountains lit beige under a navy dusk sky (dusk sky still too dark).
- Session 2026-10-10 11:00 UTC (ran in parallel with the 10:00 one; rebased onto it):
  - **Afternoon sky no longer navy (was Next 1).** The PhysicalSky is calibrated for a
    ~1.0 sun and ours is 3.0, so the sky (and its reflections) rendered far too dark.
    `sky_energy_scale` default 1.0 -> 2.6: clear blue at noon and 16:30.
  - Sky material is written only when energy/stars actually change (each write restarts
    the incremental radiance bake).
  - `tools/shots.sh` forwards extra words to shot.gd, so the diagnostic flags work:
    `tools/shots.sh "village v 30 16.5 nosdfgi"` (before, only the hour got through).
- Session 2026-10-10 10:00 UTC:
  - **Fixed "everything in shadow renders pure black"** (top visual bug). Diagnosed with
    480x270 test shots (`--resolution 480x270`, 20 frames, ~2 min each — use these for
    A/B diagnostics, not full shots). Two independent zeros stacked: (1) sky-radiance
    ambient contributed ~0 in shadow (x4 ambient energy changed nothing); (2) SDFGI, which
    replaces the ambient term, also gave ~0 indirect light on lavapipe. Either alone kept
    shadows black. Fix in `world/world.gd`: ambient is now a time-of-day colour (blue-grey
    skylight warming toward dusk, moonlit blue at night); SDFGI auto-disables when the
    adapter is llvmpipe/lavapipe (`sdfgi_allowed` export for a future quality slider).
    SSAO/SSIL still on. Fog no longer washes the whole sky grey (`fog_sky_affect` 0.4→0.12,
    volumetric 0.3→0.1).
  - `shot.gd` diagnostics: `novol`, `norefl`, `skyrt`, `noupd`, `skyx=N`, `ambx=N`.
    Note: `noupd` freezes the clock at world's default 17.25 h, not the requested hour.
- Session 2026-10-10 09:00 UTC:
  - **Black-oak groves and mossy boulders** (`world/flora.gd`, all procedural): 4 oak
    variants (gnarled forking trunk → limbs → twigs as tapered knobbly tubes with root flare
    and bark UVs; ~1000 leaf-sprig cards per tree from a painted 1024² oak-leaf atlas,
    crown-shaped normals, back-light), 260 trees scattered in noise groves, 9 m spacing;
    6 boulder variants (noise-displaced, plane-cut, flattened icospheres), 420 rocks in
    slope outcrops + lone stones. MultiMesh, shadows on, trunk/boulder collision.
    Shaders: `bark` (seamless furrow noise + normal, moss on top/roots), `leaves` (alpha
    test, flutter), `rock` (strata/cracks/lichen/moss, damp foot), shared
    `tree_wind.gdshaderinc` so trees sway with the grass gusts.
  - Village floor (r < 38 m) and the diagonal road to the east ridge are kept clear.
  - `tools/bots.sh` runs each bot once (it ran twice) and FAILs on any SCRIPT ERROR (it
    used to PASS with a broken script); bots now also assert flora scattered.
  - `tools/shots.sh` timeout 240→600 s: with trees a Vulkan shot takes ~170 s at 30 frames

- Session 2026-10-10 08:40 UTC (ran in parallel with the 08:00 one; merged onto it):
  - **Fixed the "hills float as ribbons" bug at its root: the terrain mesh had inverted
    triangle winding and was back-face culled, so it was invisible from above.** The ribbons
    were the far sides of hills seen from below; everything else was the sky's ground colour.
    Every M0 verdict about a "flat brown valley" was looking at the sky. Hills now render
    with real long shadows.
  - Day/night: proper sun arc (sunrise 06:00, noon `sun_peak_deg` 28, sunset 18:00), a cool
    moon DirectionalLight opposite the sun at night, procedural star field (bound to the
    PhysicalSky only after dusk, since it is added unattenuated), fog colour/albedo/emission,
    exposure and a moonlit ambient colour all follow the time of day. Volumetric fog cut to
    local haze (a dense volume ends at its length and leaves a band on far ridges).
  - `tools/shots.sh` diagnostics: `noshadow`, `nogi` (plus `nofog`, `nosdfgi`, `noglow`).

  between clumps is flat grey-brown, horizon is empty. Alive? No (only wind).
- `village` 17h (before density pass): sparse sprouts, looked like a rice paddy -> fixed by
  the density pass.
- `side` 16.5h: grass slope good; beyond 64 m the valley is an empty brown plane, distant
  hills float as ribbons above fog. Fail.
- **Biggest visual problems now, in order:** (1) nothing but grass: no trees, rocks, village;
  (2) far terrain/horizon: hills float in fog, world ends at 256 m — needs far mountains /
  horizon ring and fog height tuning; (3) bare-ground colour under grass.

## Asset download situation (cloud box)
- **ambientcg.com, polyhaven.com/dl.polyhaven.org, huggingface.co and cdn.jsdelivr.net are
  blocked** by the egress proxy (403 on CONNECT). github.com, raw.githubusercontent.com,
  pypi.org and registry.npmjs.org work. So photoreal CC0 textures/models cannot be fetched from
  their home sites here; options for later sessions: CC0 assets mirrored in GitHub repos
  (e.g. godot-demo-projects ships Poly Haven 1k ship textures), or keep going procedural.
  Recheck each session with `curl -sI https://ambientcg.com`.

## Known bugs
- (04:00 10-29) DUPLICATE WORK again: a 04:00 session built the same roadmeet MEET_R 3 + profile lens increment and
  was dropped at rebase for main's 3cd901b (its `roadmeet2` shot from the -lft verge matched main's; bots roadmeet,
  greet, neighbour, walk passed on it). Its one finding kept: `pedlar` bot FLAKY on HEAD 3ec7c3e (before either
  roadmeet change): "the pedlar's fist pokes out of the lid's shadow" in ~2 of 3 runs (fist cover 1.1-1.5, pass
  < 1). It is real, not a sampling artefact: probing lid_cover on process_frame (after HandLantern re-hangs) gave
  1.3-1.5 every run. Spikes are brief (1-2 ticks) on legs 6 and 10, pivot yaw ~-0.35 rad, no sidesteps. Hanging
  the pivot plumb above the hand (world-up offset instead of the wrist-local (0, 0.08, 0.025)) was WORSE (2.5-3.5),
  so the hand marker's local axes are what keeps the bail over the fist. Next ideas: damp _sway when the hand
  accelerates hard, or widen the lid a little, then re-look at a dusk `pedlar` shot.
- (21:30 10-28) DUPLICATE WORK: a session started ~19:00 built the deer road crossing a second time (herd.gd crossing
  with its own RNG, `deercross` bot + shot spot); it was dropped at rebase for main's a637833. Check `git log origin/main`
  right after setup AND again before starting code, since sessions now overlap by an hour or more.
- (21:30 10-28) MEMORY / SHOT TIME: the session cgroup OOM-kills Godot when two Forward+ shots run with bots at once
  (the dusk `pedlar ... moths` render was at 11.6 GB RSS when killed; `dmesg | grep -i killed` shows it, and shots.sh
  then just says "vulkan failed ... retrying opengl3"). A day `deercross ... fixfps` shot run ALONE with
  SHOT_TIMEOUT=4200 then overran 70 min with nothing saved. One shot at a time, no bots while it renders, and expect
  Forward+ day shots to need more than 70 min now.
- (18:40 10-28) A parse error in tools/shot.gd makes shots.sh burn its whole SHOT_TIMEOUT before saying anything (the SCRIPT ERROR only shows when the run ends). Check shot.gd with `--check-only` before every shot.

  bark climb uses local VERTEX.y, so it scales with the tree.
